l1.sections.0.blocks.12.lines.1	は、彼の作品の中で特に人気がある。これは2003年にアカデミー賞を取った
l1.sections.0.blocks.12.lines.2	映画だ。アニメに興味がある人[[なら|2]]、「もののけ{姫|ひめ}」や「となりのトトロ」も見
l1.sections.0.blocks.12.lines.3	たことがあるかもしれない。彼のアニメのストーリーには環境問題のメッセー
l1.sections.0.blocks.12.lines.4	ジがあって、大人も考えさせられるものが多い。
l1.sections.0.blocks.12.lines.5	¶宮﨑監督は、{髪|かみ}とひげが白くて、黒いふちのメガネをかけている。笑顔の監
l1.sections.0.blocks.12.lines.6	督は優しそうに見えるが、映画を作る時にはとても{厳|きび}しくなる。例えば、自分
l1.sections.0.blocks.12.lines.7	が言う[[とおりに|3]]{描|か}けるまでスタッフに何度も絵を直させる。スタッフが描いた
l1.sections.0.blocks.12.lines.8	肉の絵を見て、「この肉はゴムのようだ。かたいのか、やわらかいのか、考えて
l1.sections.0.blocks.12.lines.9	描きなさい」と怒ったこともある[[らしい|4]]。また、たった4秒のシーンを1年以
l1.sections.0.blocks.12.lines.10	上かけて作ったこともあるそうだ。
l1.sections.0.blocks.12.lines.11	¶「はずかしい作品は作りたくない」と監督は言っている。だから、いい作品を
l1.sections.0.blocks.12.lines.12	作る[[ために|5]]、朝から晩までほとんど休まないで仕事をする。昼ご飯と晩ご飯も
l1.sections.0.blocks.12.lines.13	5分で食べる。テレビや{趣味|しゅみ}に大切な時間を使わない。若い時は、朝9時から
l1.sections.0.blocks.12.lines.14	次の日の朝5時まで仕事をしていたそうだ。このように自分にも厳しいから、
l1.sections.0.blocks.12.lines.15	美しくて芸術的なアニメが作れるのだ。
l1.sections.0.blocks.12.lines.16	¶絵もストーリーもすばらしい彼の作品は、いつまでも世界中で愛され続ける
l1.sections.0.blocks.12.lines.17	だろう。

l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.0	¶「iPS{細胞|さいぼう}」という言葉を聞いたことがありますか。
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.1	iPS細胞というのは、体のいろいろな部分になること
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.2	ができる細胞です。近い将来、iPS細胞で難しい病気
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.3	の人を助けられる[[ようになる|7]]と考えられていて、「夢の
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.4	細胞」とも言われています。この細胞を作ったのが、研
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.5	究者の{山中|やまなか}{伸|しん}{弥|や}教授です。彼は2012年、50歳の時に、
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.6	iPS細胞の研究でノーベル賞を受賞しました。
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.7	¶山中教授は1962年生まれで、出身は{大阪|おおさか}です。彼
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.8	は学生時代に{柔道|じゅうどう}やラグビーをしていて、何回もけがを
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.9	しました。そのこと[[がきっかけで|6]]、けがをした人を助け
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.10	られる医者になろうと決心しました。しかし、病院で働
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.11	き始めた時、病気をなおす[[ための|5]]方法がなくて苦しんで
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.12	いる人がたくさんいるのを知って、ショックを受けまし
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.13	た。それが、医者をやめて研究をしようと思った理由の
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.14	一つだったそうです。
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.15	¶山中教授は明るい性格でおもしろい人です。講演では
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.16	必ず1回は笑わせる[[ようにしている|8]]そうです。例えば、
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.17	医者だった時にした手術の話があります。{患者|かんじゃ}は山中教
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.18	授の友人でした。15分ほどの簡単な手術だったのですが、
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.19	山中教授は手術が苦手なので、1時間以上かかってしま
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.20	いました。それで、手術中の友人に「すまん」とあやまり
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.21	ました。声が聞こえた友人は、「すまんって……」と不安
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.22	になったそうです。
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.23	¶教授[[によると|9]]、「人生の目標は、iPSの技術をベッド
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.24	サイドに{届|とど}けて、多くの患者を{救|すく}うこと」だそうです。教
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.25	授がアメリカ留学中に学んで大切にしている「VW」と
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.26	いう言葉があります。これは、研究で結果を出す[[ために|5]]
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.27	はV (Vision) とW (Work hard)、つまり、「目標を決め
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.28	てがんばる」ことが必要だという意味です。「9回{失敗|しっぱい}し
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.29	ないと1回{成功|せいこう}しない。失敗するのは、はずかしいこと
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.30	ではない」という言葉[[どおり|3]]、山中教授はiPS細胞が
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.31	できるまで決して研究をやめませんでした。これが、彼
l1.sections.0.blocks.15.lines.32	が多くの人に尊敬されている理由なのかもしれません。
l1.sections.0.blocks.16.title	{名詞|めいし}{修飾|しゅうしょく}
l1.sections.0.blocks.16.en	Noun modification
l1.sections.0.blocks.16.blocks.0.text.en	Sentences that contain noun-modifying clauses are much easier to decipher if you’re able to accurately parse their structure. The key here is to understand where the noun-modifying clause starts and ends.
l1.sections.0.blocks.16.blocks.1.title	ストラテジー
l1.sections.0.blocks.16.blocks.1.blocks.0.items.0.en	Noun-modifying clauses always come before the modified noun, and their predicate is in the plain form. If you find a verb right before a noun, it is very likely the end of a noun-modifying clause.
l1.sections.0.blocks.16.blocks.1.blocks.0.items.1.en	The subject of a noun-modifying clause is typically marked with が rather than は, so look for a が that might help you figure out where the noun-modifying clause starts.
l1.sections.0.blocks.16.blocks.2.text.ja	{名詞|めいし}{修飾|しゅうしょく}{部|ぶ}を見つける
l1.sections.0.blocks.16.blocks.2.text.en	Identifying the noun-modifying clause
l1.sections.0.blocks.16.blocks.3.text.ja	(a) {田中|たなか}さんは　パーティーで　日本語を　**__勉強している__**　%%人%%に会った。
l1.sections.0.blocks.16.blocks.4.text.ja	(b) 田中さんは　パーティーで　**__日本語を__**　**__勉強している__**　%%人%%に会った。
l1.sections.0.blocks.16.blocks.5.labels.0	Noun
l1.sections.0.blocks.16.blocks.5.labels.1	×
l1.sections.0.blocks.16.blocks.6.items.0.en	勉強している is a plain-form verb placed right before the noun 人, so we know that it is the predicate of a noun-modifying clause.
l1.sections.0.blocks.16.blocks.6.items.1.en	日本語を is the object of 勉強している, so it has to be part of the noun-modifying clause. What about the パーティーで before that? Since parties aren’t usually an occasion for studying Japanese, we can assume that it is not part of the noun-modifying clause.
l1.sections.0.blocks.16.blocks.7.text.ja	{助詞|じょし}「が」に注目する
l1.sections.0.blocks.16.blocks.7.text.en	Using the particle が as a clue
l1.sections.0.blocks.16.blocks.8.text.ja	(a) **母は**　%%作ったケーキ%%　を__食べた__。　!!➡「食べた」人＝母。「作った」人は？!!
l1.sections.0.blocks.16.blocks.9.text.ja	(b) %%**母が**　__作った__ケーキ%%　を食べた。　!!➡「作った」人＝母。「食べた」人は？!!
l1.sections.0.blocks.16.blocks.11.items.0.en	Since the particle in 母は is は, we can tell that 母 is the subject of the entire sentence predicated by 食べた. As the subject of 作った is omitted, we can assume that it is the same as the subject of the sentence, meaning 母 is also the subject of 作った.
l1.sections.0.blocks.16.blocks.11.items.1.en	Here, the particle used with 母 is が, which means 母 is the subject of a noun-modifying clause whose predicate is 作った. Since the subject of 食べた has been left out, it is likely “I,” the person making the statement.
l1.sections.0.blocks.16.blocks.12.title.ja	「肉の絵」を{修飾|しゅうしょく}する部分はどこからどこまでか。また「__{描|か}いた__」と「__見て__」の{主語|しゅご}はだれか。
l1.sections.0.blocks.16.blocks.12.title.en	Where does the clause modifying 肉の絵 start and end? What is the subject of {描|か}いた, and of 見て?
l1.sections.0.blocks.16.blocks.12.ref	（読み物1：行8〜10）
l1.sections.0.blocks.16.blocks.12.blocks.0.text.ja	スタッフが__{描|か}いた__%%肉の絵%%を__見て__、「この肉はゴムのようだ。かたいのか、やわらかいのか、考えて描きなさい」と怒ったこともあるらしい。
l1.sections.0.blocks.17.title	{指示詞|しじし}
l1.sections.0.blocks.17.en	Demonstrative words
l1.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.0.text.en	Words starting with こ, そ or あ that serve as simple substitutes for other words (such as これ / それ / あれ, この / その / あの, and ここ / そこ / あそこ) are called demonstrative words. Once you determine the referent of a demonstrative word in a sentence, you’ll have an easier time understanding how that sentence relates to the preceding one.
l1.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.1.title	ストラテジー
l1.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.1.blocks.0.items.0.en	In most cases, demonstrative words point to something that closely precedes them in the text, so when you need to figure out what a demonstrative word is referring to, carefully scan the words that come before it. Once you think you’ve found the referent, test it by seeing whether the sentence makes sense when that word or phrase is used in place of the demonstrative word.
l1.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.1.blocks.0.items.1.en	Keep in mind that demonstrative words sometimes refer to an idea encapsulated in longer text, such as the entire preceding sentence or paragraph.
l1.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.2.text.ja	「それ」が{指|さ}すものを見つける
l1.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.2.text.en	Identifying the referent of それ
l1.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.3.text.ja	週末、カレーを食べた。**__それ__**は、　彼女が初めて作ってくれた料理だった。
l1.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.text.ja	(a) %%それ%%は、　彼女が初めて作ってくれた__料理__だった。　!!それ＝料理!!
l1.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.5.text.ja	↓
l1.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.6.text.ja	(b) 週末、__カレー__を食べた。%%それ%%は、　彼女が初めて作ってくれた__料理__だった。　!!料理→カレー!!
l1.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.7.text.ja	↓
l1.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.8.text.ja	(c) 週末、カレーを食べた。__%%カレー%%は、彼女が初めて作ってくれた料理だった__。　!!それ＝カレー!!
l1.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.10.items.0.en	In the sentence **(a)**, the portion 彼女が初めて作ってくれた is a clause modifying 料理. This means that the main part of this sentence is それは料理だった, and thus we know that the referent of それ is some form of 料理.
l1.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.10.items.1.en	Search for words associated with 料理 that precede それ. カレー seems to be the best choice. **(b)**
l1.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.10.items.2.en	Next, test it out by replacing それ with カレー. **(c)**
The sentence makes sense, so we can safely assume that カレー is the referent.
l1.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.11.text.ja	{指示詞|しじし}が文を{指|さ}す{場合|ばあい}もある
l1.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.11.text.en	Sometimes the demonstrative word refers to the preceding sentence
l1.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.12.text.ja	__{昨日|きのう}カラオケに行った__。%%その%%時、好きなアニメソングを歌った。
l1.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.14.title.ja	「__それ__」が{指|さ}すものは何か。
l1.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.14.title.en	What does それ refer to in the following?
l1.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.14.ref	（読み物2：行11〜15）
l1.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.14.blocks.0.text.ja	病院で働き始めた時、病気をなおすための方法がなくて苦しんでいる人がたくさんいるのを知って、ショックを受けました。__それ__が、医者をやめて研究をしようと思った理由の一つだったそうです。

l1.sections.0.blocks.19.blocks.1.items.2.lines.1.sp	B
l1.sections.0.blocks.19.blocks.1.items.2.lines.1.ja	いいですね。日本の冬**といえば**、温泉ですよね。
l1.sections.0.blocks.19.blocks.2.forms.0	N !!といえば!!
l1.sections.0.blocks.19.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.0.en	N といえば X states the speaker’s opinion that X is the first thing that comes to his/her mind when he/she thinks of N.
l1.sections.0.blocks.19.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.1.en	This structure is also used when a speaker quotes a part of the statement made by their conversation partner in order to bring up a new—but related—topic (e.g., “speaking of which,” “speaking of N”).
l1.sections.0.blocks.20.pattern	〜なら

l1.sections.0.blocks.21.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.0.en	X とおり (に) means “exactly the same way as X.” とおり (に) is used when X is a verb, and どおり (に) is used when X is a noun.
l1.sections.0.blocks.21.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.1.en	X can be instructions (e.g., “if you make [curry] ''following the directions I give you''” in [#1]), or a source of information (e.g., “just as I heard in a rumor”).
l1.sections.0.blocks.21.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.2.en	Common verbs used in X are verbs that express one’s opinion and/or statement, such as 言う, 話す, 聞く, 思う and 考える. Some examples of nouns used in X are {予定|よてい}, 計画, {希望|きぼう}, {想像|そうぞう}, {予想|よそう}, {練習|れんしゅう}, レシピ and マニュアル.
l1.sections.0.blocks.21.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.3.en	The particle に can be used only when X とおり/どおり explains the manner in which the action expressed by the following verb is performed (as shown in [#1], [#2] and [#4]). In other cases, に should be omitted ([#3]).

l1.sections.0.blocks.22.blocks.3.blocks.0.items.0.en	〜らしい is used to convey information the speaker learned from another source by reading or hearing about it.
l1.sections.0.blocks.22.blocks.3.blocks.0.items.1.en	〜らしい and 〜そうだ for hearsay can be used interchangeably.

l1.sections.0.blocks.22.blocks.3.blocks.2.items.0.en	However, while 〜そうだ is used to report exactly what the original source stated, 〜らしい includes inferences made by the speaker as well.
l1.sections.0.blocks.22.blocks.3.blocks.2.items.1.en	When the source of the information is judged to be unreliable (such as the rumor used in [#2]), 〜らしい is more commonly used than 〜そうだ.
l1.sections.0.blocks.22.blocks.3.blocks.2.items.2.en	〜らしい is also used when the speaker did not hear the original statement directly from the source (such as the case in [#3]) or the speaker did not hear explicit statement (as in [#4]), but the speaker makes an inference based on what he/she heard.

l1.sections.0.blocks.25.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.0.en	〜ようになる expresses a change. When the verb is in dictionary form, as in [#1] and [#2], it means the subject of the sentence previously did not do the action but came to do it habitually. When the potential form of verb is used, as seen in [#3] through [#5], it expresses that the action was not possible before, but now is.
l1.sections.0.blocks.25.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.1.en	Since the structure ようになる expresses a change, verbs that inherently describe a change (e.g., {太|ふと}る, やせる, {慣|な}れる, {増|ふ}える) cannot be used with this structure.

l2.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.items.1.en	Expressions for broaching the subject of a letter or e-mail are not placed at the beginning. Instead, they usually come after an opening paragraph containing a seasonal greeting or the writer’s personal news. Also, the main message is typically followed by a closing line or paragraph wishing the recipient good health or fortune. The closing is often marked by では.

l2.sections.0.blocks.18.title	お願いやお礼の{表現|ひょうげん}
l2.sections.0.blocks.18.en	Expressing requests and gratitude
l2.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.0.text.en	Japanese letters and e-mail often employ certain set expressions to convey requests or gratitude to superiors.
l2.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.1.title	ストラテジー
l2.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.1.blocks.0.items.0.en	Look for set expressions and use them as a clue to determine what the writer is requesting or offering thanks for.
l2.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.2.text.ja	目上の相手にお願いする{表現|ひょうげん}
l2.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.2.text.en	Expressions for making a request to a superior
l2.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.3.items.0.ja	ご{都合|つごう}を教え**ていただけないでしょうか**。
l2.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.3.items.1.ja	ご都合を教え**ていただけるとうれしいです**。
l2.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.3.items.2.ja	来週までにご都合を教え**ていただければ{大丈夫|だいじょうぶ}です**。
l2.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.4.items.0.en	Examples **(a)** and **(b)** are both expressions used to convey （教え）てほしい in the form of a request, but differ in tone. 〜ていただけないでしょうか in **(a)** is a relatively direct way to make this request to a superior. 〜ていただけるとうれしいです in **(b)** makes for a softer and thus more polite request. For some nouns, いただく itself is used as a verb to mean “humbly receive,” as in お時間をいただけるとうれしいです.
l2.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.4.items.1.en	〜ていただければ{大丈夫|だいじょうぶ}です in **(c)** expresses the writer’s minimum need, and can be used to reduce the mental pressure placed on the recipient.
l2.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.5.text.ja	目上の相手への{感謝|かんしゃ}を表す{表現|ひょうげん}
l2.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.5.text.en	Expressions for thanking a superior
l2.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.6.items.0.ja	日本語を教え**てくださって／ていただき、ありがとうございました。おかげさまで／**先生**のおかげで**、試験に{合格|ごうかく}する**ことができました。**
l2.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.6.items.1.ja	試験に合格する**ことができたのは**、先生**のおかげです。本当にありがとうございました。**
l2.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.7.items.0.en	〜てくださってありがとうございました in **(a)** is used to thank a superior. The おかげ that follows is used to express gratitude for a specific success made possible by the superior’s support. 〜てくださって can be substituted with 〜ていただき. **(b)** is an example of how to thank the superior again at the end of the letter/e-mail.
l2.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.8.title.ja	お願い、またはお礼の{表現|ひょうげん}に、下線を{引|ひ}きなさい。
l2.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.8.title.en	Underline the expressions for making a request or conveying gratitude.

l2.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.2.forms.0	Vて → V~~ます~~、〜。

l2.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.0.en	The stem in Vます form is a formal equivalent of 〜て.
l2.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.1.en	As seen in examples [#3], [#4] and [#5], formal equivalents for derivatives of て-forms would be [#Vて]おり for [#Vて]いて, [#Vず]に for [#Vない]で, and せずに for しないで.
l2.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.3.text.en	☛ い{形容詞|けいようし} て-form のフォーマルな{表現|ひょうげん}〈Formal expression of て-form of い-adjectives〉
l2.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.4.text.en	In the case of て-form of い-adjectives (〜くて), the formal expression would be 〜く.

l2.sections.0.blocks.21.blocks.8.blocks.0.items.0.en	[#Vて]いく expresses that a change is currently taking place (or will soon begin to take place) and the change is expected to continue into the future.
l2.sections.0.blocks.21.blocks.8.blocks.0.items.1.en	Similarly to [#Vて]くる, verbs that inherently express changes (e.g., {太|ふと}る, やせる, {増|ふ}える, {減|へ}る, なる, 慣れる) are used in [#Vて]いく, and when other types of verbs are used, the structure would be modified to [#Vる]ようになっていく, as shown in [#7].

l2.sections.0.blocks.23.blocks.2.forms.0	Vる／Vない !!ことにする!!
l2.sections.0.blocks.23.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.0.en	〜ことにする is used to state the subject’s own decision or determination regarding their action in the future.
l2.sections.0.blocks.23.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.1.en	〜ことにしている describes an action that the subject decided to do and is now continuing as a routine.
l2.sections.0.blocks.23.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.2.en	Verbs in the potential form cannot be used in place of Vる or Vない.
l2.sections.0.blocks.23.blocks.3.text.en	☛ 〜ようにしている (make sure to do) and 〜ことにしている (make it a rule to do)
l2.sections.0.blocks.23.blocks.4.text.en	〜ようにしている conveys that the subject is making a conscious effort to do something as much as he/she can, whereas 〜ことにしている conveys that the subject has a firm determination to do something consistently.

l2.sections.0.blocks.23.blocks.6.items.0.en	I make it a rule to drink 1 liter of water every day.

l2.sections.0.blocks.25.blocks.3.forms.0.stack.2	＊N~~だ~~ → の
l2.sections.0.blocks.25.blocks.3.forms.0.join	!!おかげで!!
l2.sections.0.blocks.25.blocks.3.blocks.0.items.0.en	X おかげで Y is used when there is a cause and effect relationship between X and Y, with X being the cause and Y being the effect. Y is a desirable state resulting from X (such as an act of kindness), and this structure is used typically to express gratitude toward X. Y is often something that is otherwise not controllable by the speaker.
l2.sections.0.blocks.25.blocks.3.blocks.0.items.1.en	When someone did a favor for the speaker and the speaker wants to express gratitude, [#Vて]くれた (or [#Vて]くださった) おかげで is used.
l2.sections.0.blocks.25.blocks.3.blocks.0.items.2.en	When the causality is not obvious but it is speculated that X is the cause for Y, X おかげか Y is used.

l2.sections.0.blocks.25.blocks.7.forms.0	!!おかげさまで!!
l2.sections.0.blocks.25.blocks.7.blocks.0.items.0.en	おかげさまで is used as a set phrase, often in greetings. It is not meant to state the cause for the speaker’s current state, but rather to simply express that the speaker is appreciative of his/her present condition. This phrase can be used even when the listener has not done anything in particular for the speaker.
l2.sections.0.blocks.25.blocks.7.blocks.0.items.1.en	[#6] shows a usage where おかげさまで is followed by an explicit statement of the result that the speaker had wanted.

l2.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.3.blocks.0.items.0.en	This structure states that something that will occur in the future has been decided or agreed on. It implies that the decision or agreement has been made by someone other than the speaker (e.g., another person, an organization, or society in general), and the decision maker is typically not made clear in the context.
l2.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.3.blocks.0.items.1.en	It could also be used when the speaker was involved in the decision-making process, but it was ultimately decided not just by the speaker, but in conjunction with another person or party (such as the marriage in [#3]).

l2.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.9.text.en	する expresses an action or a condition that is related to the speaker’s will, whereas なる is not related to the speaker’s will. Given this distinction between する and なる, refer to the table below for an overview of structures covered thus far:

l2.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.10.rows.0.0.text	ように
l2.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.10.rows.0.1.en	① 〜ようにする (keep in mind to do)
Used for something you make an effort to do.
l2.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.10.rows.0.2.en	③ 〜ようになる (start doing)
Used for a change of habit/situation or ability.
l2.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.10.rows.1.0.text	ことに
l2.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.10.rows.1.1.text.en	② 〜ことにする (decide to do)
Used for something you decide yourself.
l2.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.10.rows.1.2.en	④ 〜ことになる (be decided that)
Used for something decided by another person or something that naturally comes to be the case.
l2.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.10.rows.2.0.en	⑤ 〜ことになっている (it is expected that)
Used for something that has been decided as a rule or plan.

l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.0	¶日本にいる[[うちに|1]]一度訪れてほしい場
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.1	所は、[[何と言っても|2]]{富士山|ふじさん}です。富士山は
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.2	3,776メートルの日本一高い山で、{山梨|やまなし}
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.3	県と{静岡|しずおか}県の県境にあります。日本人[[に|3]]
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.4	[[とって|]]特別な山で、日本の象徴[[として|4]]愛
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.5	されており、千円札にも{描|えが}かれています。
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.6	2013年に世界文化{遺産|いさん}に登録された[[ため|5]]、外国からの登山客も多くなってき
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.7	ています。
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.8	¶これから、留学生のみなさんに富士登山に役立つ情報をご紹介します。
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.9	#登山期間とルート
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.10	¶まず、いつ、どんなルートを登るのがいいのでしょうか。一般的な登山期間
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.11	は7月{上旬|じょうじゅん}から9月上旬までの約2カ月です。この間は山小屋が開いてい
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.12	て、登山バスも走っています。登山ルートは全部で4つあり、歩く{距離|きょり}や難
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.13	しさなどがルート[[によって|6]]違います。よく調べて自分に合ったルートを選び
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.14	ましょう。
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.15	#登山計画
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.16	¶次に、登山にはどのぐらい時間が
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.17	かかるのでしょうか。例えば、登る
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.18	人が最も多い「{吉田|よしだ}ルート」では、
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.19	山頂まで約6時間かかります。その
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.20	[[ため|5]]、1日目は山頂に近い山小屋に
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.21	泊まり、次の日の朝、山頂で日の出
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.22	を見てから下山するのが一般的です。この日の出のことを「ご{来光|らいこう}」と言いま
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.23	す。山頂から美しいご来光を見れば、一生の思い出になるでしょう。
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.25	¶また、旅行会社の登山ツアーに参加すればガイドが案内してくれるので、初
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.26	めて登る人でも安心です。富士登山は人気があるので、ツアーや山小屋はなる
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.27	べく早く予約しておきましょう。
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.28	#服装
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.29	¶登山の計画を立てたら、次は服です。登山にはどのような服を着ていく[[べき|7]]
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.30	でしょうか。夏だ[[からといって|8]]、雪が降らない[[とは限りません|9]]。山頂は0度以
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.31	下になることがあるので、ダウンジャケットやセーターなどを忘れないように
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.32	してください。それに、{丈夫|じょうぶ}な登山{靴|ぐつ}も、岩が多い山頂の近くを歩く[[のに|10]]必要
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.33	です。安全のために必ず用意しておきましょう。
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.34	#注意点
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.35	¶その他には、どのようなことに気をつけたらいいでしょうか。富士山にはト
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.36	イレが少ないので、見つけたらその時に行っておいたほうがいいでしょう。ま
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.37	た、富士山にゴミ箱はないので、自分のゴミは必ず持ち帰ってください。山を
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.38	汚さないようにすることは、登山客が守る[[べき|7]]ルールです。
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.39	¶富士山には、日本だけでなく世界中から人が集まります。準備をしっかり行
l3.sections.0.blocks.14.lines.40	い、マナーを守って、日本一の山に登る感動を味わってください。

l3.sections.0.blocks.17.title	居酒屋 〜日本らしさが感じられる場所〜
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.0	¶「安い値段でおいしい料理やお酒を楽しみたい」「友達
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.1	と仲よくなる機会を作りたい」、そして「日本らしさを感
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.2	じたい」。そんな時は居酒屋に行くとよい。特にチェーン
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.3	の居酒屋は数が多く、学生や会社帰りのサラリーマン、
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.4	{主婦|しゅふ}、家族連れなど、様々な客に人気がある。人々は何を
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.5	求めて居酒屋に行くのだろうか。その特徴から居酒屋の
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.6	{魅力|みりょく}を考えてみたい。
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.7	¶一つめの特徴は、料理もお酒も種類が多いことだ。席
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.8	に着き、メニューを見れば、そのことにすぐ気がつくだ
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.9	ろう。サラダやデザートがあるのは普通の飲食店と変わ
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.10	らないが、えだ{豆|まめ}やから{揚|あ}げなど、「おつまみ」と{呼|よ}ばれ
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.11	るお酒に合う料理がたくさんある。また、ビールやワイ
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.12	ンの他に、日本酒やチューハイなど、いろいろなお酒が
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.13	注文できる点も異なる。ジュースやお茶などのソフトド
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.14	リンクもあるので、お酒が飲めない人でも楽しむことが
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.15	できる。
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.16	¶二つめの特徴[[として|4]]考えられるのは、その安さだ。料
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.17	理も飲み物も安い[[ため|5]]、注文する時に値段を気にしなく
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.18	ていい。だから、あまりお金がない時でも安心して食べ
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.19	たり飲んだりできる。さらに、お酒を飲む量が多い人は、
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.20	「飲み放題」というシステムを覚えておくといい。「飲み
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.21	放題」では決まった金額を払えば、90分などと決められ
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.22	た時間内に何種類ものお酒が好きなだけ飲める。
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.23	¶しかし、[[何と言っても|2]]最大の特徴は、居酒屋が交流の
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.24	場になっていることだ。どの店にもたいてい個室がある
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.25	ので、居酒屋はグループで集まる[[のに|10]]とても{便利|べんり}だ。例
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.26	えば、大学のサークルの「飲み会」や会社のプロジェク
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.27	トやイベント後の「打ち上げ」でよく使われる。年末の
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.28	「忘年会」や年始の「新年会」なども開かれる。そういう
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.29	集まりでみんなでわいわい話せば、疲れている時の息抜
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.30	きやストレス解消にもなる。また、あまり話したことが
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.31	なかった人と親しくなれるチャンスにもなる。
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.33	¶このように、おいしい料理やお酒が安く楽しめ、交流
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.34	が深められる場所——それが居酒屋だ。あなたもぜひ一
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.35	度、居酒屋に足を運んで、その独特な{雰囲気|ふんいき}を感じてみ
l3.sections.0.blocks.17.lines.36	てほしい。

l3.sections.0.blocks.20.title	{段落|だんらく}の{要旨|ようし}を表す文
l3.sections.0.blocks.20.en	Sentences describing the gist of a paragraph
l3.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.0.text.en	Expository writings are made up of interrelated paragraphs. By identifying the gist of each paragraph, you can see how it relates to the surrounding paragraphs, and thus get a handle on the organization of the text as a whole.
l3.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.1.title	ストラテジー
l3.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.1.blocks.0.items.0.en	To understand the gist of a paragraph, look for a question presenting a topic and its anwer, or a sentence summarizing the paragraph.
l3.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.2.text.ja	問いかけ文とその答え
l3.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.2.text.en	A question introducing a paragraph’s topic and its answer
l3.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.3.lines.0	¶日本で自然を楽しみたい人は、__どこに行くといいでしょうか__。日本には自然が美しい
l3.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.3.lines.1	場所がたくさんありますが、もし海が好きなら、__{沖縄|おきなわ}がいいでしょう__。沖縄の海の色は
l3.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.3.lines.2	……
l3.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.4.items.0.en	As in the example above, questions introducing a paragraph’s topic（どこに行くといいでしょうか）are often placed at the beginning. The key message of the paragraph is provided by the response to the question（{沖縄|おきなわ}がいいでしょう）, so if you find a question and its answer in a paragraph, you can use them to work out the main idea of that paragraph.
l3.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.5.text.ja	中心文
l3.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.5.text.en	Topic sentences
l3.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.6.lines.0	¶{京都|きょうと}は{観光客|かんこうきゃく}にとても人気がある町だ。これから京都の特徴を紹介したい。
l3.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.6.lines.1	¶[[京都の最大の特徴は、日本の歴史が感じられることだ|a]]。[[1,000年以上、日本の{首都|しゅと} (capital)|b]]
l3.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.6.lines.2	[[だったおかげで、古いお寺や神社が多い。また、和食や昔からの祭りなどの日本の{伝統|でんとう}|]]
l3.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.6.lines.3	[[文化も楽しめる|]]。
l3.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.7.items.0.en	Sentence **(a)** is a topic sentence summarizing the content of the second paragraph. l3.sections.0.blocks.29.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.0.en	X からといって Y ない states that X is not a good enough reason to conclude that Y is true, even though people might believe that X can be a reason for Y. For instance, [#5] implies that one might assume that a rich person must be happy, but wealth alone does not equate with happiness, and there may be people who are rich but unhappy.
l3.sections.0.blocks.29.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.1.en	Some common forms used in Y ない are [#Vて]はいけない, 〜とは限らない and 〜わけではない (see Lesson 6).
l3.sections.0.blocks.29.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.2.en	In casual settings, an abbreviated form 〜からって instead of 〜からといって is used, as in [#2].

l3.sections.3.blocks.18.lines.0.sp	
l3.sections.3.blocks.18.lines.0.ja	週末、日本人の友達と晩ご飯を食べに行きました。その友達が「食べたいラーメンがある」と言うのでそのお店に行ったのですが、私たちが行った時には、もうたくさんの人が{並|なら}んでいました。お店の人に聞いたら、「1時間ほどかかります」と言うのです。そんなに待ちたくなかったので、私は「別の店に行こう」と言ったのですが、友達が「絶対にここで食べたい」と言うので、{結局|けっきょく}{行列|ぎょうれつ}に並ぶことになりました。
l3.sections.3.blocks.18.lines.1.sp	
l3.sections.3.blocks.18.lines.1.ja	待っている間に、友達に、「日本人は待つのが好きだよね。本当によく行列を見るよ」と言ったら、「外国の人も並んでるよ。何か新しいものが発売される時は、どこでもたくさん並んでるでしょう？　例えば、スマートフォンとか、テレビゲームとか。それに、イタリアに旅行に行った時、ピザの店で{結構|けっこう}待たされたよ」と言われました。

l4.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.48	先的に採用します。つまり、3年生の時に海外に1年間留学すると、

l4.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.51	くことが必要だと思います。

l4.sections.0.blocks.17.title	{強調構文|きょうちょうこうぶん}「XのはYだ」
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.en	Emphatic construction: XのはYだ
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.0.text.en	X のは Y だ is a sentence pattern commonly used in Japanese writing to emphasize something. Sentences using this construction play a key role in compositions.
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.1.title	ストラテジー
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.1.blocks.0.items.0.en	Look for X のは Y だ sentences and determine what they are emphasizing.
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.2.text.ja	{強調構文|きょうちょうこうぶん}のパターン
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.2.text.en	Emphatic sentence construction
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.3.text.ja	__私__は　__{土曜日|どようび}__に　__すし__を　食べた。
(a)　(b)　(c)
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.head.0.0.ja	{強調|きょうちょう}するもの (what to emphasize)
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.head.0.1	
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.head.0.2	(X)
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.head.0.3	
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.head.0.4	(Y)
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.head.0.5	
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.rows.0.0.ja	(a) **私** !!（だれが）!!
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.rows.0.1	→
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.rows.0.2.ja	__土曜日にすしを食べた__
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.rows.0.3	のは
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.rows.0.4.text.ja	私
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.rows.0.5	だ。
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.rows.1.0.ja	(b) **土曜日** !!（いつ）!!
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.rows.1.1	→
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.rows.1.2.ja	__私がすしを食べた__
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.rows.1.3	のは
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.rows.1.4.text.ja	土曜日
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.rows.1.5	だ。
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.rows.2.0.ja	(c) **すし** !!（何を）!!
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.rows.2.1	→
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.rows.2.2.ja	__私が土曜日に食べた__
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.rows.2.3	のは
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.rows.2.4.text.ja	すし
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.rows.2.5	だ。
l4.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.5.items.0.en	In this emphatic construction, the noun being emphasized is embedded in the predicate (〜だ), and the rest of the sentence is placed before 〜のは (here, the subject of the non-emphatic construction, 私は, is replaced with 私が, as seen in **(b)** and **(c)**).

l4.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.5.text.ja	主語や「に」がつく人が{省略|しょうりゃく}された場合
l4.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.5.text.en	Omission of the subject or person marked with に
l4.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.6.text.ja	子どもの時、母はとてもきびしかった。毎日のように「勉強しなさい」と
__言われて__、一日に何時間も__勉強させられた__。
l4.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.7.text.ja	(a) !!{受身形|うけみけい} → 言った人は「母」!!　(b) !!{使役受身形|しえきうけみけい} → 勉強した人は「私」!!
l4.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.8.items.0.en	**(a)** Since 言われる is passive, the agent of this action would be the one marked with に, but there is no に in this sentence. Consequently, we have to rely on the context to decide whether the words were said by 私, or by 母. In most cases, the subject of a passive sentence is 私, meaning that the writer was affected by some action done by another person, so it is likely that 私は母に has been omitted from the sentence in this example. Therefore, the person who said 勉強しなさい was the mother.
l4.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.8.items.1.en	**(b)** 勉強させられる is causative-passive, so the agent of this action would be the one marked by は. Since we already know 私は母に has been omitted from the sentence, we can tell 私 is the person who did the studying.

l4.sections.0.blocks.22.blocks.2.forms.0.lead	!!せっかく!!
l4.sections.0.blocks.22.blocks.2.forms.0.stack.0	〜（んだ）から
l4.sections.0.blocks.22.blocks.2.forms.0.stack.1	〜のに
l4.sections.0.blocks.22.blocks.2.forms.1	!!せっかく!!の N
l4.sections.0.blocks.22.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.0.en	An action or event that follows the form せっかく is considered to be a rare occasion or an opportunity.
l4.sections.0.blocks.22.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.1.en	“せっかく X（んだ）から、Y” states that one should make the most of X—a rare occasion or an opportunity—by doing Y. Y is often given in the form of advice, suggestion, request, or the speaker’s hope or resolution. Forms commonly used in Y are: 〜ほうがいい, [#Vて]ください, V たい and [#V(よ)う].
l4.sections.0.blocks.22.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.2.en	“せっかく X のに Y” conveys the speaker’s acknowledgement that X is a rare occasion or an opportunity, and expresses regret for not fully making the most of X.
l4.sections.0.blocks.22.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.3.en	When X is a noun, the particle の is used after せっかく ([#5]).

l4.sections.0.blocks.23.blocks.3.blocks.0.items.0.en	N さえ emphasizes N as an extreme example.
l4.sections.0.blocks.23.blocks.3.blocks.0.items.1.en	N さえ X mentions N as an extreme example for X, and implies that since N is X, other things would be X as well.
l4.sections.0.blocks.23.blocks.3.blocks.0.items.2.en	さえ also implies the speaker’s surprise, self-deprecation, sarcasm, etc. While も can be used for similar function (i.e., to mean ''even'' in an emphatic way), さえ conveys the aforementioned feelings more than も alone.
l4.sections.0.blocks.23.blocks.3.blocks.0.items.3.en	As seen in [#1] and [#2], in sentences where N is normally followed by the particle を, が, は or も, the particle is omitted and さえ is used alone.

l4.sections.0.blocks.23.blocks.3.blocks.4.items.0.en	As in [#5], when さえ is attached to the nominative case (i.e., a subject that is also an agent of a verb), でさえ is used instead of さえ.

l4.sections.0.blocks.23.blocks.7.blocks.0.items.0.en	“X ば Y” expresses that a desirable result Y would be achieved if a condition X is met. By adding さえ to a word or phrase in X, this structure would convey that other conditions are not necessary as long as X is met. The statement in [#1] means that the speaker is content as long as he/she has some chocolate, and he/she doesn’t need anything else.
l4.sections.0.blocks.23.blocks.7.blocks.0.items.1.en	さえ can also follow a verb in stem form or て-form. In the case of a verb stem, すれば would follow さえ, whereas a 〜て form（ている）would be converted to 〜てさえいれば.

l4.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.0.en	X ば Y のに expresses the speaker’s regret that X is not the case, because if X were realized, it would be Y (the desired result). X ばいいのに is a common set phrase used to express such regret when the speaker feels X is not and ''will'' not be realized.

l4.sections.3.blocks.14.lines.1.sp

l4.sections.3.blocks.14.lines.2.sp	
l4.sections.3.blocks.14.lines.2.ja	インターンシップ先の社員の人にどうして長い休みを取らないのか聞いたら、「自分だけ休むのは{同僚|どうりょう}に申し訳ない」とか、「自分がいないとできない仕事があるから、{周|まわ}りに{迷惑|めいわく}がかかる」と言っていました。昔と比べたらずいぶん休みが取りやすくなったそうですが、たいていの人は1日の休みを時々取るだけだそうです。長い休みを取れ__ば__家族とゆっくり__旅行できるのに__、と思いますが、日本ではプライベートより仕事を優先する人が多いようです。

l5.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.0	¶以前、すしには高級なイメージしかなかった。そのイメージを変えたのが、
l5.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.1	回転ずしだ。回転ずしの店では、一皿100円から数百円という安い値段ですし
l5.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.2	が食べられる。安くてもネタが新鮮でおいしいので、人気店は[[いつ|1]]行っ[[ても|]]込
l5.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.3	んでいる。「一皿100円」という看板を見たら、つい入ってしまう人もいるだ
l5.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.4	ろう。
l5.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.5	#回転ずしとは？
l5.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.6	¶回転ずしの店に入ると、テーブルのす
l5.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.7	ぐ横を回る大きなベルトコンベアがま
l5.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.8	ず目に入ってくる。そのベルトコンベア
l5.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.9	で次々と運ばれてくるおいしそうなす
l5.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.10	しの中から、客は食べたいものを自由に
l5.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.11	取って食べる。ネタの種類は豊富で、季
l5.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.12	節限定のネタや洋風の変わったネタもあるので、行く[[たびに|2]]楽しめる[[はずだ|3]]。
l5.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.13	また、すし以外にラーメンやデザートなどのサイドメニューを出す店も増えて
l5.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.14	きている。
l5.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.15	¶そして、回転ずしは通常、皿の色ごとに値段が決まっている。だから、メ
l5.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.16	ニューがなく値段がわからない高級なすし屋とは違って、回転ずしなら安心し
l5.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.17	て好きなものを選ぶことができる。

l5.sections.0.blocks.22.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.0.en	“(もし)X ても、Y”states that Y will be realized in spite of X, implying that normally Y would not be realized if X is the case. l5.sections.3.blocks.15.lines.0.sp	
l5.sections.3.blocks.15.lines.0.ja	日本に留学して3カ月になり、仲がいい日本人の友達もできました。その友達は同じ授業を取っているので、授業の後に晩ご飯を食べに行ったりカラオケに行ったりします。
l5.sections.3.blocks.15.lines.1.sp	
l5.sections.3.blocks.15.lines.1.ja	この間、その友達の家の近くを通ったので、{遊|あそ}びに行ってみることにしました。ところが友達は、「今はちょっと無理。遊びに来る時は、前もって{連絡|れんらく}してもらえないかな？」と言って、中に入れてくれませんでした。
l5.sections.3.blocks.15.lines.2.sp	
l5.sections.3.blocks.15.lines.2.ja	授業の後で遊ぶ時は前もって{約束|やくそく}しなくてもいいのに、どうして家に遊びに行く時は約束が必要なのかわかりませんでした。それで、他の日本人の友達に聞いてみたら、「その時忙しかっただけかもしれないけど、人の家に行く時は、約束し__ておいた__ほうがいいよ。お客さんが来る前にそうじしたい人もいるし」と言われました。
l5.sections.3.blocks.15.lines.3.sp	
l5.sections.3.blocks.15.lines.3.ja	約束をし__ておかない__と家に遊びに行けないのは{面倒|めんどう}だと思うのですが、日本では約束をし__ておいた__ほうがいいのでしょうか。みなさんの国ではどうですか。

l6.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.9	¶しかし、最近気になることがあり
l6.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.10	ます。それは、{過|か}{剰|じょう}な包装です。例
l6.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.11	えば、箱に入ったクッキーを買った
l6.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.12	ら、その箱が紙で包まれていただけ
l6.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.13	ではなく、中のクッキーも一枚ずつ
l6.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.14	ビニールで包装されていました。確
l6.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.15	かに一つ一つ袋に入っていれば箱か
l6.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.16	ら取る時に手が汚れないし、すぐに

l6.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.18	す。しかし、ゴミは確実に増えま
l6.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.19	す。それに、箱の包装紙も、後で使
l6.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.20	えない[[わけではない|1]]ですが、結局捨
l6.sections.0.blocks.13.lines.21	てる人が多いので、資源のむだです。

l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.0	#早期英語教育に賛成？反対？
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.1	¶英語でコミュニケーションができる日本人を育てよう
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.2	と、日本の英語教育制度が大きく変わり始めている。
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.3	小学校での英語の授業はすでに全国的に始まっている
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.4	が、今後も早期化が進むと考えられ、早期英語教育に
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.5	ついて人々の関心がますます高くなっている。今の大
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.6	学生はこの現状をどのように考えているのだろうか。
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.7	#英語は子どものうちに
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.8	@{北東|ほくとう}大学 {青山|あおやま} {絵|え}{理|り}（21）
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.9	¶私は、早期英語教育に賛成だ。理由は三つある。
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.10	¶第一に、言語の習得はある年齢を超えると難しくなる
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.11	からだ。言語を学ぶ年齢と習得には深い関係があるとよ
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.12	く言われる。スポンジのように何でも吸収できる子ども
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.13	のうちに言語を学び始めれば、楽に習得できる。特に発
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.14	音の習得に効果的だ。例えば、多くの日本人が苦手な英
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.15	語の「L」と「R」の発音も、小さい時から学べば、よく
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.16	なる[[のではないだろうか|3]]。
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.17	¶第二に、子どもは英語に対する抵抗感を持たないから
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.18	だ。小学校でゲームや歌を通して学べば、子どもは恥ず
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.19	かし[[がらず|4]]に英語を話すことができる。それに、新しく
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.20	覚えた言葉をすぐに使お[[うとする|5]]ので、早く慣れること
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.21	もできる。一般的に、人は大人になればなるほど周りの
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.22	目を意識し、失敗を恐れる気持ちが強くなる。だから、そ
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.23	の気持ちが生まれる前に英語学習を始めることが重要だ
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.24	と思う。
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.25	¶第三に、国際感覚を早く身につけることができるから
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.26	だ。子どもの時に外国語を学び、自国とは違う文化があ
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.28	ることに気づくことで、異なる価値観を受け入れやすく
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.29	なると言われている。小学生のうちに英語を通して外国
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.30	の文化や習慣に触れ、視野を広げておけば、将来、国際的
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.31	に活躍できる可能性が広がる[[のではないか|3]]と思う。
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.32	¶このように、早期英語教育はメリットが多いので、積
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.33	極的に進めていくべきだ。
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.35	#子どもに英語は必要か
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.36	@{西川|にしかわ}大学 {木|き}{村|むら} {準|じゅん}（19）
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.37	¶私は早期英語教育には反対だ。その理由を三つ述べる。
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.38	¶まず、母語である日本語の発達に悪い影響がある。日
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.39	本語が不十分な[[まま|6]]英語の勉強を始めると、どちらも正
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.40	しく使えなくなってしまう可能性がある。自分の考えを
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.41	一つの言語でうまく表現できず、混乱してしまうことも
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.42	あるだろう。だから、英語を学ぶのは自分の考えや感情
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.43	が日本語で論理的に伝えられるようになるまで待つべき
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.44	な[[のではないだろうか|3]]。
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.45	¶また、自分の意思で英語を学んでいる子どもは少ない。
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.46	私の場合も子どもの時、親に英語の{塾|じゅく}に行く[[ように言わ|7]]
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.47	[[れ|]]、いやいや勉強させられた。その経験[[からいうと|2]]、英語
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.48	を習いた[[がって|4]]いない子どもに無理にやらせても、意味
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.49	がない。英語嫌いな子どもが増えるだけだ。子どもが英
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.50	語に興味を持ち始めるまで待ったほうがいいと思う。
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.51	¶さらに、日本では日常生活で英語が必要だ[[というわけ|1]]
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.52	[[ではない|]]。子どもの時に英語を学んでも、ふだん使わな
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.53	ければ、すぐに忘れてしまうだろう。勉強を始めるのは、
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.54	英語が本当に必要になった時でも遅くはないと思う。私
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.55	の先輩に英語が全然話せなかった人がいたが、仕事でカ
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.56	ナダに行くことになってから本気で勉強し始め、驚く[[ほ|8]]
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.57	[[ど|]]上達した。この例からも、[[わざわざ|9]]子どものうちに英
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.58	語を学ばなくてもいいと言えるだろう。
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.59	¶以上のような理由から、私は早期英語教育は必ずしも
l6.sections.0.blocks.16.lines.60	必要ないと主張したい。
l6.sections.0.blocks.17.title	意見を述べる時の表現
l6.sections.0.blocks.17.en	Expressions for conveying opinions
l6.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.0.text.en	When reading a statement of opinion, look for the following two expressions that are commonly used to convey opinions.
l6.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.1.title	ストラテジー
l6.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.1.blocks.0.items.0.en	If you come across the pattern “確かに〜。しかし〜。”, note that the writer’s opinion will follow the しかし.
l6.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.1.blocks.0.items.1.en	In the pattern “X のではないだろうか”, the part represented by X is the writer’s opinion.
l6.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.2.text.ja	確かに〜。しかし〜。
l6.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.3.text.ja	留学中にホームステイをする人もいるし、寮に住む人もいる。どちらのほうがいいのだろうか。**確かに**ホームステイで日本の家族と生活するのはいい経験になるだろう。**しかし**、ホームステイの場合、大学から遠いところに住むことが多く、通学に時間がかかるのが問題だ。だから、キャンパスに近い寮に住んだほうがいいと思う。
l6.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.items.0.en	As indicated in the question asked, the writer is discussing the relative advantages of homestays versus dormitories. Before stating his opinion, the writer uses 確かに to present a benefit of doing a homestay, and then immediately uses しかし (but) to counter with a disadvantage of homestays. This construction shows that the writer does not favor homestays.
l6.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.4.items.1.en	The しかし in the pattern “確かに〜。しかし〜。” is sometimes replaced with another word that means “but,” such as でも or けれども.
l6.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.5.text.ja	Xのではないでしょうか
l6.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.6.text.ja	__8歳の子どもに{携帯|けいたい}電話は必要ない__**のではないでしょうか。**
(X)
l6.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.7.items.0.en	The writer’s opinion in the example above is the underlined part.
l6.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.8.title.ja	{筆者|ひっしゃ}の意見の部分に下線を{引|ひ}きなさい。
l6.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.8.ref	（読み物1：行11〜20）
l6.sections.0.blocks.17.blocks.8.blocks.0.text.ja	例えば、箱に入ったクッキーを買ったら、その箱が紙で包まれていただけではなく、中のクッキーも一枚ずつビニールで包装されていました。確かに一つ一つ袋に入っていれば箱から取る時に手が汚れないし、すぐに全部食べなくてもいいので便利です。しかし、ゴミは確実に増えます。
l6.sections.0.blocks.18.title	{列挙|れっきょ}の表現
l6.sections.0.blocks.18.en	Expressions for enumeration
l6.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.0.text.en	When writers enumerate a list of things, such as when stating the various reasons behind an opinion, they often start by saying how many items will be mentioned.
l6.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.1.title	ストラテジー
l6.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.1.blocks.0.items.0.en	If a writer states that there is a certain number (like 2 つ or 3 つ) of reasons, examples, etc., expect that number of items to follow.
l6.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.1.blocks.0.items.1.en	Look for expressions such as まず, また, 第一に or 第二に to keep track of the discrete items that make up a list or group.
l6.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.2.text.ja	「まず」「また」を使って理由を{列挙|れっきょ}する
l6.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.2.text.en	Enumerating reasons using まず and また
l6.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.3.text.ja	私は大学生はアルバイトをしたほうがいいと思う。==理由は2つある==。**まず**、__アルバイトをすると、学生以外の人と知り合う機会が増える__。違う年代の人や様々な経験を持つ人と付き合うことで、視野が広がるのではないだろうか。**また**、__時間の使い方も学ぶことができる__。勉強と仕事のバランスを取るのは難しいが、うまく時間が使えるようになれば、社会に出てからきっと役に立つだろう。
l6.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.4.items.0.en	The statement 理由は 2 つある makes it clear that two reasons will be enumerated. Using this clue, look for two expressions of enumeration and you’ll find まず and また. Each signifies that one of the reasons will immediately follow, so now you can readily make out the two items.
l6.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.5.title.ja	下の質問に答えなさい。
l6.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.5.ref	（読み物2：行10〜19）
l6.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.5.blocks.0.text.ja	私は、早期英語教育に賛成だ。理由は三つある。
l6.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.5.blocks.1.text.ja	第一に、言語の習得はある年齢を超えると難しくなるからだ。言語を学ぶ年齢と習得には深い関係があるとよく言われる。スポンジのように何でも吸収できる子どものうちに言語を学び始めれば、楽に習得できる。特に発音の習得に効果的だ。例えば、多くの日本人が苦手な英語の「L」と「R」の発音も、小さい時から学べば、よくなるのではないだろうか。
l6.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.5.blocks.2.text.ja	第二に、子どもは英語に対する抵抗感を持たないからだ。……
l6.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.5.blocks.3.items.0.text.ja	{筆者|ひっしゃ}はこれからこの{文章|ぶんしょう}全体（行8〜33）で、何の理由をいくつ{列挙|れっきょ}するか。
l6.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.5.blocks.3.items.0.text.en	How many reasons are going to be listed in the entire passage? What are the reasons for?
l6.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.5.blocks.3.items.1.text.ja	一つ目の理由は何か。その部分に下線を{引|ひ}きなさい。
l6.sections.0.blocks.18.blocks.5.blocks.3.items.1.text.en	What is the first reason? Underline it.

l6.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.2.forms.1.stack.2	＊N（だ）
l6.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.2.forms.1.join	!!というわけではない!!
l6.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.0.en	The form X わけではない is used to deny X, a statement that may be inferred or assumed based on what is known in context or what has previously been stated. [#1] means, “Joining a calligraphy club does not immediately result in improved skills in calligraphy (even though one might make such an assumption).”
l6.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.1.en	[#4] shows 〜わけではない used in double negative construction, turning the statement into a positive one. This example would be translated as “I ''can'' cook, (although I may not be good at it.)” As [#5] shows, in the case of N, it is often used in the form of N（だ）というわけではない rather than N なわけではない.
l6.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.2.en	As [#1] shows, わけではない is often used with からといって, a structure covered in Lesson 3.
l6.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.3.text.en	☛ 〜わけではない and 〜とは限らない（L3-9）
l6.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.4.text.en	わけではない and とは限らない have overlapping meanings, and can sometimes be interchangeable.
l6.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.5.label	例1）
l6.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.5.items.0.ja	日本人がみんな魚が好きな__わけではない__。
l6.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.5.items.0.en	(In general Japanese like fish, but) this does not mean that all Japanese like fish.
l6.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.5.items.1.ja	日本人がみんな魚が好きだ__とは限らない__。
l6.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.5.items.1.en	It is not always the case that all Japanese like fish.
l6.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.6.text.en	There are cases, however, when the two structures are not interchangeable. X わけではない is used to negate a statement X, while X とは限らない simply implies that there is a possibility that X is not true. As such, X とは限らない structure cannot be used when X is a statement about yourself that you already know is not true.
l6.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.7.label	例2）
l6.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.7.items.0.ja	私はよくカラオケに行くが、歌が得意な__わけではない__。
l6.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.7.items.0.en	I often go to Karaoke, but it does not mean that I am good at singing.
l6.sections.0.blocks.20.blocks.7.items.1.ja	私はよくカラオケに行くが、歌が得意だ__とは限らない__。

l6.sections.0.blocks.21.blocks.2.forms.0.lead	N !!から!!
l6.sections.0.blocks.21.blocks.2.forms.0.stack.0	!!みると!!
l6.sections.0.blocks.21.blocks.2.forms.0.stack.1	!!すると!!
l6.sections.0.blocks.21.blocks.2.forms.0.stack.2	!!いうと!!
l6.sections.0.blocks.21.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.0.en	N からみると, N からすると and N からいうと all make a statement about something from the standpoint of N.
l6.sections.0.blocks.21.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.1.en	In the case of N からみると, N usually takes words or phrases that represent a particular person or a group of people, making a statement that speaks for N’s opinion or views.
l6.sections.0.blocks.21.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.2.en	N in N からすると takes information that is used as evidence or grounds for making a judgment.
l6.sections.0.blocks.21.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.3.en	N からいうと is used when the speaker wants to acknowledge that there are many different ways to look at the matter mentioned, but the speaker is commenting on it from just one of those perspectives. Unlike N からみると or N からすると, N からいうと cannot take nouns representing people in N.

l6.sections.0.blocks.22.gloss	(although I cannot say this with confidence,) I think it is the case that ~

l6.sections.0.blocks.22.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.0.en	X のではないだろうか expresses the speaker’s opinion or speculation about something without sounding assertive. As such, this structure is often used when eliciting an agreement from a listener in a subtle manner, or when disagreeing with someone in a non-confrontational way.
l6.sections.0.blocks.22.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.1.en	X のではないだろうか conveys level of confidence that is lower than that of X だろう.
l6.sections.0.blocks.22.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.2.en	In conversation, X んじゃないでしょうか is used in a formal setting, and X んじゃない？ is used in a casual setting. ([#3])
l6.sections.0.blocks.22.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.3.en	When used with な-adjectives and nouns, なの in なのではないだろうか may be omitted. ([#4] and [#5])
l6.sections.0.blocks.22.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.4.en	When 〜と思う follows this structure, だろう is omitted and 〜（の）ではないか is used. ([#5])

l6.sections.0.blocks.23.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.0.en	This structure describes a third person’s outward (though not necessarily verbal) expression of having certain feelings or desires. Examples of adjectives commonly used with this structure are さびしい, 恥ずかしい, うらやましい, 痛い, 残念だ, {嫌|いや}だ, ほしい and V たい.
l6.sections.0.blocks.23.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.1.en	In this structure, the object of desires or feelings takes the particle を. As shown in [#4] and [#5], when ほしい or V たい is used, the desired item or action takes the particle を, rather than が.
l6.sections.0.blocks.23.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.2.en	This structure typically takes the ている form (i.e., 〜がっている), but it appears in the 〜がる form when making a comment about someone’s tendency to desire something ([#6]), as opposed to an observed instance of someone desiring something.
l6.sections.0.blocks.23.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.3.en	As described above, 〜がる or 〜がっている has connotations that the person mentioned is showing ''outward'' signs of feelings or desires, suggesting that he/she does not have good control of their own emotions. Therefore, making a statement in this structure about someone superior to you should be avoided.

l6.sections.0.blocks.25.blocks.7.forms.0	Vた!!まま!!
l6.sections.0.blocks.25.blocks.7.blocks.0.items.0.en	[#Vた]まま expresses that the state that resulted from an action V is left unchanged.
l6.sections.0.blocks.25.blocks.7.blocks.0.items.1.en	[#V₁た]まま V₂ expresses that someone is doing V₂ while maintaining the same state that resulted from V₁.
l6.sections.0.blocks.25.blocks.7.blocks.0.items.2.en	Typically, V₁ in [#V₁た]まま V₂ takes momentary verbs (i.e., verbs for actions that can be completed in an instant) such as 開ける, {閉|し}める, 立つ, 座る, つける, 消す, 着る and 出かける.
l6.sections.0.blocks.25.blocks.7.blocks.0.items.3.en	The agent of the two actions (V₁ and V₂) must be the same; in [#5], the one who turned the light on and the one who fell asleep is identical (person B, in this case).
l6.sections.0.blocks.25.blocks.7.blocks.0.items.4.en	[#Vた]まま cannot be used in negative form (V なかったまま). The present negative form ([#Vない]まま) can be used, and it conveys that the action is not done ''for some length of time'' (i.e., it is left incomplete or not initiated). To simply express that the action is not done, without placing emphasis on the passage of time, [#Vず]に or [#Vない]で should be used.

l6.sections.0.blocks.26.blocks.2.forms.0	Vる／Vない !!ように言う!!
l6.sections.0.blocks.26.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.0.en	ように言う / 注意する / 頼む / お願いする are used to indirectly quote requests or commands (e.g., 〜てくれませんか, 〜てください, 〜なさい).
l6.sections.0.blocks.26.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.1.en	When quoting a request that was expressed by the speaker or the subject of the sentence, and it was directed towards a third person, 〜てくれるように is used more commonly than 〜ように, as seen in [#4].
l6.sections.0.blocks.26.blocks.3.text.en	☛ 〜ように注意する
l6.sections.0.blocks.26.blocks.4.text.en	〜ように has two functions: (1) to indirectly quote a request or a command, and (2) to express a purpose behind an action. The latter function was covered in Lesson 5 Grammar 7. When 注意する follows 〜ように, function (1) conveys advice or warning (例 1), whereas function (2) expresses being cautious or paying attention in order to achieve a particular purpose (例 2).
l6.sections.0.blocks.26.blocks.5.label	例1）
l6.sections.0.blocks.26.blocks.5.items.0.ja	私はルームメートに部屋のかぎを忘れない__ように注意した__。
l6.sections.0.blocks.26.blocks.5.items.0.en	I told (advised) my roommate not to forget the room key.

l6.sections.0.blocks.27.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.0.en	X ほど Y uses X as an example (either factual or metaphorical) to emphasize the high level of Y. Typically, X takes verbs in potential forms or intransitive verbs, as well as adjectives followed by なる.
l6.sections.0.blocks.27.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.1.en	{飛|と}び上がるほど（うれしい）, {涙|なみだ}が出るほど（うれしい） and 死ぬほど（忙しい） are idiomatic phrases that are commonly used for emphasis.
l6.sections.0.blocks.27.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.2.en	This expression X ほど Y may also be seen in reversed order Y て、X ほどだ to convey the same idea. [#3] can be rephrased as 最近、テレビを見る時間もないほど忙しい to mean the same thing. However, 死ぬほど in [#4] would only be used in X ほど Y structure.

l6.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.1.items.3.lines.1.sp	先生
l6.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.1.items.3.lines.1.ja	**わざわざ**取りに行かなくても、明日でいいですよ。
l6.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.2.forms.0	!!わざわざ!!
l6.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.0.en	わざわざ implies that someone went out of their way or spent their time and effort to do something when it was not necessary.
l6.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.2.blocks.0.items.1.en	わざわざ is often used in conjunction with expressions of appreciation for someone’s unsolicited act of kindness that required time and/or effort ([#3]). It is also used to decline an offer or preempt someone’s action by acknowledging that the action is not necessary ([#4]).
l6.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.3.text.en	☛ わざわざ and せっかく（L4-3）
l6.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.4.text.en	わざわざ and せっかく both imply that the subject of the sentence is intentionally taking the trouble (i.e., going out of their way) to do something; however, the two words have a subtle difference in their connotation. The difference is that わざわざ conveys that the speaker considers the action to be something unnecessary, whereas せっかく is used when the speaker thinks of the action as an opportunity or a special occasion, which they feel should not be wasted.
l6.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.5.label	例1）
l6.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.5.items.0.ja	__わざわざ__ケーキを作ったのに、彼は食べてくれなかった。
l6.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.5.items.0.en	Even though I took the trouble to bake a cake (when I did not need to spend my time and effort to bake one), he did not even eat it.
l6.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.6.label	例2）
l6.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.6.items.0.ja	__せっかく__ケーキを作ったのに、彼は食べてくれなかった。
l6.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.6.items.0.en	Even though I took the trouble to bake a cake (as I thought it would make him happy), he did not even eat it (and I’m sad that my thoughtfulness, time and effort were wasted).
l6.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.7.text.en	せっかく is also limited in that it can only be used with particular expressions such as 〜のに and 〜から, while there is no restriction for structures to be paired with わざわざ.
l6.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.8.label	例3）
l6.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.8.items.0.ja	友達に服を貸したら、__わざわざ__クリーニングをして返してくれた。
l6.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.8.items.0.en	When I lent some clothes to a friend, she took the trouble to get them dry-cleaned before returning them to me.
l6.sections.0.blocks.28.blocks.8.items.1.ja	友達に服を貸したら、__せっかく__クリーニングをして返してくれた。

l6.sections.3.blocks.23.items.0.blocks.0.items.0.text.ja	メイリンは、暑い日に{窓|まど}を{閉|し}めるのを忘れて出かけてしまった。

l6.sections.3.blocks.23.items.0.blocks.0.items.1.text.ja	ルームメートはメイリンに、「窓を閉めたほうがいいんじゃない？」と言った。

l6.sections.3.blocks.23.items.0.blocks.0.items.2.text.ja	ルームメートは、窓を開けたまま出かけてもいいと思っていた。

l6.sections.3.blocks.23.items.1.text.ja	メイリンは、ルームメートの「窓が開いていたよ」にはどんな意味があったと考えていますか。
l6.sections.3.blocks.23.items.1.answer.ja	「{閉|し}めて」という注意の意味があったと考えています。
l6.sections.3.blocks.23.items.2.text.ja	メイリンは、どうしてはっきり言ってほしいと思っていますか。
l6.sections.3.blocks.23.items.2.answer.ja	はっきり言ったほうが{誤解|ごかい}がなくて、お{互|たが}いに気持ちよく過ごせるからです。
l6.sections.3.blocks.24.text.ja	ディスカッション
l6.sections.3.blocks.25.items.0.text.ja	あなたが相手に言いにくいと思うのはどんなことですか。
l6.sections.3.blocks.25.items.1.text.ja	はっきり言えない時、あなたはどうやって言いたいことを伝えますか。
l6.sections.3.blocks.25.items.2.text.ja	あなたは、下の①と②の{状況|じょうきょう}の時、何と言いますか。はっきり言う場合とあまりはっきり言わない場合の言い方を考えてみましょう。
l6.sections.3.blocks.25.items.2.blocks.0.items.0.text.ja	暑いのでエアコンの温度を下げたいが、友達は寒いと思っているようだ。
l6.sections.3.blocks.25.items.2.blocks.0.items.1.text.ja	友達が家に{遊|あそ}びに来ている。もう遅いので、そろそろ帰ってもらいたい。
l6.sections.3.blocks.26.key.0	1. ① ×　② ○　③ ×　④ ○　⑤ ○
l6.sections.3.blocks.26.key.1	2. 「{閉|し}めて」という注意の意味があったと考えています。
l6.sections.3.blocks.26.key.2	3. はっきり言ったほうが{誤解|ごかい}がなくて、お{互|たが}いに気持ちよく過ごせるからです。
l6.sections.3.blocks.26.intro.ja	{中国人|ちゅうごくじん}留学生のメイリンが、クラスで「注意する時の言い方」についてスピーチをしています。スピーチを聞いて質問に答えなさい。
l6.sections.3.blocks.26.lines.0.sp	
l6.sections.3.blocks.26.lines.0.ja	この間、日本人のルームメートの言いたいことがわからなくて、少し嫌な思いをしました。その日は暑かったので、私は{窓|まど}を開けて出かけました。部屋に帰った時、ルームメートに「出かける時は窓を{閉|し}めたほうがいい__んじゃない__？」と言われたのですが、私はただのアドバイスだと思い、あまり気にしていませんでした。そして、次の日も暑かったので、窓を開けた__まま__出かけました。その晩ルームメートは少し怒って「{昨日|きのう}、窓を閉めてから出かける__ように頼んだ__のに、なんで窓を開けた__まま__出かけたの？」と言いました。ルームメートの「閉めたほうがいい__んじゃない__？」というのは「閉めて」という注意の意味だったのです。
l6.sections.3.blocks.26.lines.1.sp	
l6.sections.3.blocks.26.lines.1.ja	考えてみると、家に帰ってきたらルームメートに「また窓が開いていたよ」と言われたことが前に何度かありました。これも「閉めて」という意味だったのでしょうか。

l6.sections.3.blocks.26.lines.2.ja	注意する時は少し言いにくくても、はっきり言ってくれたほうが{誤解|ごかい}がなくて、お{互|たが}いに気持ちよく過ごせると思います。みなさんだったら、ルームメートにどのように注意しますか。

g1.title	書き{言葉|ことば}の{文体|ぶんたい}
g1.en	Styles in written Japanese
g1.blocks.0.text.en	Written and spoken Japanese differ in styles. Written Japanese is further divided into two subcategories: です・ます style and だ・である style. These two styles are used in different contexts, and need to be used appropriately according to their form and function.
g1.blocks.1.title.ja	次の (1) 〜 (3) はどのような{文章|ぶんしょう}に使いますか。
a. レポート、b. 先生へのメール、c. {友達|ともだち}へのメールの中から{選|えら}んでください。

g1.blocks.4.text.en	です・ます style (polite style) refers to the style in which nouns and adjectives are followed by です and verbs are written in ます form. This style shows respect towards the audience, while conveying a soft tone of voice (as if the writer is speaking to the reader). It is often used in letters and e-mail messages addressed to someone superior to the writer.
g1.blocks.5.text.en	On the other hand, だ style (plain style) takes nouns/ な-adjectives followed by だ and verbs/い-adjectives in plain forms. The plain style in written language is considered more formal than です・ます style, and is often used in written reports, theses, articles in newspaper, and other compositions in which the writer may need to convey ideas in a concise and assertive manner.
g1.blocks.6.items.0.en	In casual writing addressed to the general publice (such as blog articles and essays), either です・ます style or だ style may be used.
g1.blocks.6.items.1.en	In each writing, the same style should be used consistently; です・ます style and だ style are not to be mixed.
g1.blocks.6.items.2.en	In だ style, だ after nouns/な-adjectives can be replaced by である style (literary style). である style is used only when emphatic expression is needed.
g1.blocks.7.text.ja	話し{言葉|ことば}と書き言葉
g1.blocks.7.text.en	Spoken vs. written Japanese
g1.blocks.8.text.en	The plain form is also used in spoken Japanese, but this usage is considered casual. Expressions specific to speech, such as the examples in the table below, should not be used for the plain style in writing, as they differ significantly in the level of formality.

g3.blocks.2.text.en	Honorific form
g3.blocks.3.text.en	Honorific forms are used when making statements about someone who is of higher status than the speaker. They express that the speaker regards the person highly.
g3.blocks.4.labels.0	Regard someone highly
g3.blocks.4.labels.1	{尊敬語|そんけいご}
g3.blocks.5.text.ja	{尊敬動詞|そんけいどうし}の3つの{形|かたち}
g3.blocks.5.text.en	Three subcategories of honorific verbs
g3.blocks.6.text.en	Special verbs
g3.blocks.7.items.0.ja	先生は今オフィスに__いらっしゃいます__。（います）
g3.blocks.8.text.ja	お＋V~~ます~~＋になる／ご〜になる
g3.blocks.9.label	例1）
g3.blocks.9.items.0.ja	先生は日本の文化について__お話しになりました__。（話しました）
g3.blocks.10.label	例2）
g3.blocks.10.items.0.ja	一度__ご{使用|しよう}になった__ものは{返品|へんぴん}できません。（使用した）
g3.blocks.11.text.ja	れる／られる
g3.blocks.11.text.en	(Same form as passive)
g3.blocks.12.items.0.ja	先生は来年日本文化のクラスを__教えられる__そうです。（教える）
g3.blocks.13.blocks.0.items.0.en	When special verbs are available, use them instead of other honorific forms. See p. 215 for a list of special verbs.
g3.blocks.13.blocks.0.items.1.en	When special verbs are not available, use “お＋V~~ます~~＋になる.” When the verb is a Sino-Japanese word (i.e., ''kango''), use “ご＋V (without する) ＋になる.”
g3.blocks.13.blocks.0.items.2.en	れる／られる is considered slightly less respectful than the first or second form, but is nevertheless an honorific form.
g3.blocks.13.blocks.0.items.3.en	The three subcategories of honorific forms should not be used simultaneously in one verb.
g3.blocks.13.blocks.1.label	話す →

g3.blocks.23.text.en	Other special expressions of honorific form
g3.blocks.24.items.0.ja	人 → {方|かた}　　家 → お{宅|たく}　　どう → いかが　　だれ → どなた　　あれ → あちら
g3.blocks.25.text.ja	{謙譲語|けんじょうご}
g3.blocks.25.text.en	Humble form
g3.blocks.26.text.en	The humble form lowers the speaker, thereby elevating the person of higher status. It is used to make statements about the speaker’s own action or objects related to the speaker.
g3.blocks.27.text.en	There are two types in humble form: Type I is a rather typical humble expression used to show respect for the person at the receiving end of speaker's action, while Type II is used to show respect for the listener.

g3.blocks.35.blocks.0.items.0.en	Humble form Type I is used when the speaker’s action somehow affects or relates to a person of higher status.
g3.blocks.35.blocks.0.items.1.en	When special verbs are available, use them instead of the humble form in ②. See p. 215 for a list of special verbs.
g3.blocks.35.blocks.0.items.2.en	When special verbs are not available, use “お＋V~~ます~~＋する.” When the verb is a Sino-Japanese word (i.e., ''kango''), use ご＋V する.

g3.blocks.38.items.0.en	(''at a restaurant'')
g3.blocks.39.blocks.0.items.0.en	Humble form Type II is used when the speaker’s action does not directly affect people of higher status, but the speaker still needs to show respect to the listener. It is commonly used in formal settings such as self-introduction, or by people working in a customer service industry. です・ます style is always used with this form. Only special verbs are available for Humble Type II.
g3.blocks.40.text.ja	ウチ／ソトと{敬語|けいご}
g3.blocks.40.text.en	In-group/out-group and polite style
g3.blocks.41.labels.0	ウチ (in-group)
g3.blocks.41.labels.1	ソト (out-group)
g3.blocks.41.labels.2	私
g3.blocks.41.labels.3	{敬意|けいい}
g3.blocks.42.blocks.0.items.0.en	The polite style is used not only for people of higher status, but also for people in an ''out-group'', meaning people outside of the speaker’s social circle or people who are not in familiar relationships with the speaker. Honorific forms are used to make statements about people in an ''out-group'', whereas the humble form or です・ます style is used to talk about people within the speaker’s ''in-group'' or people who are in familiar relationships with the speaker.

g3.blocks.43.labels.1	X社
g3.blocks.43.labels.2	川村部長

g3.blocks.43.labels.4	私
g3.blocks.43.labels.5	{同僚|どうりょう}

g3.blocks.46.blocks.0.items.0.en	When speaking about people of higher status who are in your ''in-group'', the appropriate style to use in a conversation can change depending on the situation.
g3.blocks.46.blocks.0.items.1.en	If you are having a conversation with people in your ''in-group'', honorific forms should be used to talk about people of higher status (e.g., 川村部長 in example 1). On the contrary, when your conversation partner is in an ''out-group'' (e.g., people who work for other companies), then humble forms should be used when referring to people in your ''in-group'', even when the referents hold higher statuses. In such cases, titles (e.g., 部長, さん) should also be omitted.
g3.blocks.46.blocks.0.items.2.en	Who belongs in the speaker’s ''in-group'' or ''out-group'' is not fixed, and it depends on the situation and who the conversation partner is.

g4.blocks.23.blocks.0.items.0.en	[#Vて]あげる expresses that the action done by the agent would benefit the receiver. The receiver of the action should be someone other than 私, and when 私 is the receiver, [#Vて]くれる would be used.
g4.blocks.23.blocks.0.items.1.en	In this construction using [#Vて]あげる, the particle following the receiver is the same particle that would be used with the original verb, as shown in [#1], [#2] and [#3].
g4.blocks.23.blocks.0.items.2.en	As in [#4] (スーパーに行く), when the verb does not take the receiver as its object, 〜のために would be used to specify the receiver.
g4.blocks.23.blocks.0.items.3.en	When the agent of the verb is the speaker (i.e., 私), using [#Vて]あげる may sound like the speaker is emphasizing the favor he/she has done; it may be perceived as self-approval. When the receiver is of higher social status than the speaker, it would come off as rude, even if you use [#Vて]さしあげる.

g4.blocks.27.blocks.0.items.0.en	[#Vて]くれる conveys that the action (V) done by someone else would benefit the speaker. It also expresses the speaker’s gratitude for the action.
g4.blocks.27.blocks.0.items.1.en	In this construction using [#Vて]くれる, the particle following the receiver (i.e., the speaker) is the same particle used with the original verb, as shown in [#1], [#2] and [#3].
g4.blocks.27.blocks.0.items.2.en	As in [#4] ({郵便局|ゆうびんきょく}に行く), when the verb does not take the receiver as its object, 〜のために may be used to denote the receiver (which is 私, in the case of this example).
g4.blocks.27.blocks.0.items.3.en	私に, 私の, and 私のために are often omitted.

g4.blocks.31.blocks.0.items.0.en	Sentences using [#Vて]くれる can be rephrased by using [#Vて]もらう.
g4.blocks.31.blocks.1.items.0.ja	__友達__は!!(giver)!!　__私__に!!(receiver)!!　おいしい店を教え__てくれました__。

g4.blocks.31.blocks.2.items.0.en	In the [#Vて]もらう structure, the giver always takes the particle に.
g4.blocks.31.blocks.2.items.1.en	Similar to the sentence using [#Vて]くれる, [#Vて]もらう can be used to show the speaker’s gratitude for the action, when 私 is the subject (receiver). The difference between [#Vて]くれる and [#Vて]もらう is that [#Vて]もらう implies that the action was done as a favor specifically requested by the speaker.

g5.blocks.3.text.en	Adverse passive
g5.blocks.4.blocks.0.items.0.en	When the subject is a person, passive voice sentences express more or less the same idea as the active voice counterparts (sentences where the subject performs the action). The difference between them is that the passive voice expresses the subject’s feeling of annoyance, dissatisfaction, etc. caused by someone’s action.

g5.blocks.5.items.1.lines.2.ja	？私のさいふは、どろぼうにとられました。
g5.blocks.6.text.en	＊ Using “my wallet (私のさいふ)” as the subject of a passive voice sentence sounds unnatural in Japanese; 私 (the owner of the object) should be the subject instead.
g5.blocks.7.items.0.lines.0.ja	だれかが**私**の足をふみました。

g5.blocks.8.text.en	＊ Similar to [#2], “my foot” should not be the subject of the passive voice sentence, as it sounds unnatural in Japanese; 私 should be the subject instead.
g5.blocks.9.items.0.lines.0.ja	試験の前に友達が{遊|あそ}びに来ました。(''Simply stating the fact.'')

g5.blocks.10.text.en	＊ The passive voice expresses the speaker’s annoyance, etc. even when the active voice counterpart does not explicitly mention 私 in the sentence.
g5.blocks.11.items.0.lines.0.ja	雨が{降|ふ}りました。(''Simply stating the fact.'')

g5.blocks.12.text.en	＊ It is also possible to express annoyance caused by inanimate objects (e.g., 雨 in this case).

g5.blocks.23.text.en	Compulsion
g5.blocks.24.blocks.0.items.0.en	Verbs in the causative form generally express that the action was done under compulsion. The subject of the sentence is the person who forced someone else to do the action.
g5.blocks.25.items.0.lines.0.sp	お母さん
g5.blocks.25.items.0.lines.0.ja	{野菜|やさい}を食べなさい！
g5.blocks.25.items.0.lines.1.sp	子ども

g5.blocks.26.text.en	＊ As shown in [#1] above, when the original verb takes an object followed by the particle を (e.g., 野菜を), then the person who was forced to do the action (e.g., 子ども) should take the particle に. This is a grammatical rule called ''double を constraint'', which limits the use of particle を to once per verb.

g5.blocks.27.items.1.lines.1.ja	じゃあ、今回は森**に**__行かせよう__。
g5.blocks.28.text.en	＊ If the original verb is an intransitive verb, the causee (the person who was forced to do the action) is followed by the particle を, as in [#2]. Use of the particle に is not prohibited, as shown in [#3], but in this case the sentence has the connotations that the subject (causer) considered the feeling of the person who does the action (causee). As such, example [#3] implies that 社長 is showing consideration for 森’s desire to go on the business trip.
g5.blocks.29.text.ja	感情の{誘発|ゆうはつ}
g5.blocks.29.text.en	Induction
g5.blocks.30.blocks.0.items.0.en	Causative forms of intransitive verbs expressing emotions (e.g., 笑う, {泣|な}く, 怒る, びっくりする, 心配する, {困|こま}る) do not mean that the emotions were “forced” by the causer; rather, they convey that the emotions were ''induced'' by the causer. In such cases, the causee should take the particle を.

g5.blocks.38.text.en	＊ When the speaker receives a permission to do something, either 〜てくれる or 〜てもらう can be used with the causative form, depending on who the subject is. If the person ''giving'' the permission is the subject, then 〜てくれる is used (see [#2]); if the person ''receiving'' the permission is the subject, then 〜てもらう is used (see [#3]). In both cases, the speaker’s gratitude is expressed.

g5.blocks.42.title.ja	▸ {使役形|しえきけい}と{使役受身形|しえきうけみけい}
g5.blocks.42.blocks.0.text.en	There are two ways to express the idea that someone (causer) forced someone else (causee) to do something: (1) Subject (causer) + verb in causative form, and (2) Subject (causee) + verb in causative-passive form.
g5.blocks.42.blocks.1.items.0.lines.0.sp	母

g5.blocks.42.blocks.2.items.1.ja	**子ども**はお母さんに野菜を__食べさせられました__。(causative-passive)
g5.blocks.42.blocks.3.text.en	When the speaker (私) is either the causer or causee, then 私 should generally be used as the subject of the sentence.

g6.title	{条件文|じょうけんぶん}　〜たら／〜と／〜ば／〜なら
g6.en	Conditional sentences
g6.blocks.0.text.en	たら／と／ば／なら in “X たら／と／ば／なら Y” state that X is a condition for Y to occur. These forms express slightly different connotations and are not always interchangeable; read below to review the conditional forms and their differences.

g6.blocks.14.blocks.0.items.0.en	The conditional form 〜と used in “X と Y” (Y in present tense) expresses that Y ''always'' happens when X occurs. This structure is often used for a natural phenomenon ([#1]), something that occurs automatically ([#2]), and habitual routines ([#3]).

g6.blocks.17.blocks.0.items.0.en	X ば Y (Y in present tense) conveys that a desirable result (Y) would be achieved if the condition X is met.
g6.blocks.17.blocks.0.items.1.en	As in [#3], when the construction “X なければ Y ない” is used,  X is considered a prerequisite for Y to happen.
g6.blocks.17.blocks.0.items.2.en	When X uses a verb that expresses an action or change, then Y cannot use expressions that convey the speaker’s subjective opinion or wish (e.g., 〜たい, 〜(よ)う, 〜てください, 〜たほうがいい). However, when X uses an adjective or a verb that describes a state, Y can have expressions of the speaker’s subjective opinion or wish.

g6.blocks.22.blocks.0.items.0.en	X なら means “if X is the case.” X in “X なら Y” rephrases/reiterates something that was discussed previously or something that is known in the context (e.g., a statement made by the conversation partner). Y expresses the speaker’s judgment, command, desire, or volition regarding the matter mentioned in X.
g6.blocks.22.blocks.0.items.1.en	Unlike other types of conditional sentences, 〜なら can be used for a situation in which X is achieved ''after'' Y occurred (see [#1]).
g6.blocks.22.blocks.0.items.2.en	As in [#4], それなら “in that case” is used as a set phrase to refer to the previous statement made by the conversation partner.

g7.title	{助詞|じょし}「は」と「が」
g7.en	Particles は and が
g7.blocks.0.text.en	In Japanese, the particle は marks the topic of the sentence, whereas が marks the nominative case. Both are often considered the equivalent of ''subjects'' of the sentences, but the two particles are not always interchangeable. Below is a review of differences between は and が.
g7.blocks.1.title.ja	正しい答えを{選|えら}んでください。
g7.blocks.1.blocks.0.items.0.text.ja	A：テスト　a.【は・が】いつですか。
B：金曜日です。
A：何　b.【は・が】出ますか。
B：6{課|か}の文法と{助詞|じょし}の問題が出ます。漢字　c.【は・が】出ません。

g7.blocks.2.text.ja	「は」と「が」の違い

g7.blocks.4.blocks.0.items.0.en	は in “X は” marks X as the topic of the sentence, thereby signaling that the sentence will be a statement ''about'' X. が, on the other hand, marks the subject of the sentence.
g7.blocks.4.blocks.0.items.1.en	は places focus on the part of the sentence that ''follows'' は; therefore, main points of the sentence (e.g., interrogative words, important information) come ''after'' は (see [#1]). は is also used to mark the subject of the sentence that is already made clear in the context and shared between the speaker and the listener.
g7.blocks.4.blocks.0.items.2.en	On the contrary, が places focus on the part of the sentence that ''precedes'' が. As such, important information is mentioned ''before'' the particle が (see [#2]). Also in contrast to は, が is used to mark a subject that is considered to be new information for one of the speakers.
g7.blocks.5.blocks.0.rows.0.0.text	**は**：
g7.blocks.5.blocks.0.rows.0.1.en	topic
g7.blocks.5.blocks.0.rows.0.2	**は**
g7.blocks.5.blocks.0.rows.0.3.text.en	important information
g7.blocks.5.blocks.0.rows.1.0.text	**が**：
g7.blocks.5.blocks.0.rows.1.1.text.en	important information
g7.blocks.5.blocks.0.rows.1.2	**が**
g7.blocks.5.blocks.0.rows.1.3.en	statement
g7.blocks.6.text.ja	「は」の{特徴的|とくちょうてき}な使い方：{対比|たいひ}
g7.blocks.6.text.en	Specific use of は: Making a contrast
g7.blocks.7.items.0.ja	サッカー**は**よく見ますが、{野球|やきゅう}**は**あまり見ません。
g7.blocks.7.items.1.ja	{喫茶店|きっさてん}で**は**コーヒーをあまり飲みません。
g7.blocks.7.items.2.ja	喫茶店でコーヒー**は**あまり飲みません。
g7.blocks.8.blocks.0.items.0.en	The particle は has a contrastive function as well. In [#1] above, は is used to make contrast between soccer and baseball. Because of this contrastive function of the particle, conjunctions for reverse conditions (e.g., でも, 〜が) are often used with them.
g7.blocks.8.blocks.0.items.1.en	In negative sentences, は is often used to mark the specific item that is being negated. In [#2], {喫茶店|きっさてん}で is being negated specifically, implying that the speaker may drink coffee at other places, just ''not at the café''. On the contrary, in [#3], コーヒー is being negated, meaning that the speaker may drink other types of beverages but ''not coffee''.

g7.blocks.16.blocks.0.items.0.en	“X が Y” places emphasis on X; it has the connotation that it is X (and not others) that Y applies to.
g7.blocks.16.blocks.0.items.1.en	Interrogative words and answers to questions are marked with が (e.g., どの色が and 黒が in [#1]).
g7.blocks.16.blocks.0.items.2.en	[#3] emphasizes that it is 私 (and not anyone else) who will drive; therefore, が is used with 私 to place emphasis on it.

g7.blocks.19.blocks.0.items.0.en	In general, the particle が is used within a noun-modifying clause (i.e., a clause that precedes a noun and describes the noun). See examples (a) and (b) below.

g7.blocks.19.blocks.2.items.0.en	In a sentence with both an independent clause and a dependent clause, if the subject of the dependent clause is different from the subject of the independent clause, then the subject of the dependent clause should take the particle が (see [#3]).
g7.blocks.19.blocks.2.items.1.en	Within a quote (e.g., 〜と思う, 〜と言う), the subject does not always have to take the particle が. Whether to use は or が depends on the original particle used.
